09 · The Accelerator · full edit script

Seven days, 45 videos, every word from the transcript

Become a World-Class Expert And Authority In Your Niche Accelerator · Vishen Lakhiani · MVU Tallinn, live-event transcript (3,603 lines, 56,630 words). This page is the paper edit: each video with its complete verbatim text, line-numbered against the transcript file, the cuts collapsed in place with their reasons, trims marked on the line, and strong language highlighted so one decision can be applied everywhere. Estimates use 150 words a minute.

Strong-language instances marked

Reading this script

  • Kept text is shown in full with its transcript line number. It is the spoken word as transcribed; captions should follow it, corrected only for obvious transcription errors in names (see the flags).
  • Cuts inside a video are collapsed with a scissors line and the reason. Open one to read what goes.
  • Joins mark where two non-adjacent passages meet. Cover the join with an on-screen card or a clean cut on Vishen to camera.
  • Trims are notes attached to a kept line where part of that line goes.
  • Strong language is marked. Keep, bleep or cut is one decision for the whole programme.

1Day
Day 1 · 4 videos · ~20 min

Start on the Way

"When you start on the way, the way appears."

By the end of Day 1 you have written the first email of a nine-part sequence and said the suckiest thing about your business out loud.

1.3

The Nine-Part Email Sequence: What, Why, When. Never How.

Lines 160–2351,149 words~7.7 minLanguage ×1
On-screen text
  • Nine-part email sequence
  • "Phraseology": name the free thing
  • Lesson 1: the what and the why · Lesson 2 (24 hours later): the how, a taste
  • What · Why · When → the how is what they buy
  • P.S. line under every lesson
  • Sales up 96% overnight
Editor notes

The single most complete lesson in the transcript. Keep every step of the sequence; the L-theanine example (197–207) is the transferable version for non-education products. The "repeat after me / say it louder" beats (130–131, 167) can stay as audience texture or be trimmed.

160When they signed up, they got a nine-part email sequence. Okay,
161nine parts. So I'm teaching you the entire technique right now that double sales by 96%.
162Okay, I want you to write down nine-part email sequence.
163And this email sequence took me... 30 days to write because the email was essentially a book.
164The book was called The Unlimited You.
165I didn't say sign up for a nine-part email sequence. I gave it a name.
166Okay, so you take the thing that is free and you name it. This is called phraseology.
167Repeat after me.
168Phraseology. You'll notice I name things. In my book, The Code of the Extraordinary Mind,
169What are some ideas which are names that are sticky? Bending reality. What else?
170Rules. Okay, bullshit rules. What else?
171Unfuckwithable. Yes. You name things. So I name the email sequence as if it was an actual book.
172But it was. It was genuinely good. I wrote it 30 days. No AI.
173No spelling correction either, right? I mean, there were so many grammatical errors. But it was
174good, and it was based on my study of, like, I read every book by Jose Silva, and I created this
175nine-part email sequence. Now, the email sequence looked like this. Lesson one was an intro to the
176power of the mind. You're opening them up to possibility. And in lesson one, I told them,
177tomorrow I'm going to give you the CD. But I'm not going to give it to you now. I have to give it
178to you tomorrow. Because now I need you to understand what your mind is capable of,
179and I need you to get ready to check your inbox tomorrow. Why do you think I did that?
180Anticipation. Now it's more valuable. Because if you just make something free, it's not valuable.
181So I decided to take the free thing and make them wait 24 hours. The very next day, they get the
182centering exercise. Because look, I figured this out early. If I just gave them the free
183meditation, the free audio, they wouldn't listen because it was free.
184This is a weird thing about human nature. When something is too easy to get,
185we assume it's not valuable. When something is harder to get, we assume it's valuable.
186That's why in dating, people play this stupid game, playing hard to get.
187So you don't want to be too easy. You want to tease a little. That first email is the tease.
188You make them wait 24 hours. But here's the other thing. The first email uses a very,
189very interesting psychology. You're giving them the what. Your mind is a powerful tool.
190With creative visualization and at the alpha level of mind, you can achieve your goals.
191You can help accelerate healing. You can tap into intuition. Why do you want this? Because if you
192do this, life becomes easier. How? Uh-uh, you got to wait.
193So your first email gives the what and the why. You can also give the when.
194Do this every morning. But you do not give the how. I want you to write that down.
195You give the what, the why, but not the how. In marketing,
196this is called educating the user. You're educating the user on why your product matters.
197This is so important. If you're selling supplements, let's say you're selling L-theanine, okay?
198You need to educate. Anything that's new, you need to educate the user. So if you were selling L
199-theanine, which helps your body relax, your sales page isn't going to give away the L-theanine.
200They have to buy that. But your sales page or your sequence must talk about the what. What is L
201-theanine? It's a pure source. Okay, we've sourced this from mountains in Mongolia.
202Why? Because L-theanine helps your body relax. Why is it important to help your body relax?
203Because studies show that if you nap in the afternoon and you're more relaxed in the afternoon, you
204sleep better at night. And if you sleep better at night, you're healthier and your life is magical.
205And when do you take it? 2 p.m. every day after lunch.
206Now, so the what? The why, the when. But the how,
207you order. So with education products, the mistake people make is in the freebie,
208they give away the how. Okay, so we didn't give away the how immediately. But we also didn't want
209to completely leave the customer dangling. So the silver set had incredible CDs.
210We decided we are going to give the how. but 24 hours later, and only a tiny part of the how.
211We're going to give one out of 20-something meditations, but we're going to give the best one.
212Okay, so the how comes later. You can choose to completely not give the how, or you can give the
213how, but you make them dance a little. So that was lesson two. Now, the rest of the lessons,
214lesson three, before they got lesson three, they got a did you listen reminder.
215And why is that important? Because half the people still didn't listen on day two. So on day three,
216we reminded them. On day four, again, if you did listen, listen again. Lesson three is coming
217tomorrow. Then day five, lesson three comes. Now, lesson three, four,
218five, six, seven, eight, nine. Each one of those lessons,
219what they do is they would talk about the what, the why,
220and the when of the seven, principles that Silva gives you. Mind-body healing.
221What is that? Why is that important? When do you apply mind-body healing?
222Intuition. What is intuition? Why is it important? When do you tap into your intuition?
223Manifesting. What is manifesting? Why is that important? When do you manifest? So can you see how
224that worked? And after every lesson, and at the bottom of every lesson, there would be a PS line.
225Go here to get 20% off right now. So lesson four was in creative visualization.
226What is creative visualization? Why is it important? When do you do it? That's it. That's the
227principle. P.S. We go really deep in creative visualization in chapter two of Silva.
228There's a unique technique that Jose Silva engineered after working with a million graduates. Get
229chapter two here. Sorry, get the full course here 20% off. What, why, when?
230For the how, click to buy. And we kept repeating that dance. By lesson nine,
231people were like, my God, I have all of these things in my life that's missing. I need to buy this
232course. And after lesson nine, okay, we would send one,
233two, three emails that would all drive them to the sales page. And overnight,

235So can you see how you can do this?

Day 1 exercise (closing card)

Write Email 1 of your own nine-part sequence: the what, the why, the when. Not the how. Then tell one person the suckiest thing about your business right now, with a smile.

852designing this. Okay, so let's talk about the course blueprint.

872presenting, right? Or stage fright or something like this. The next section could be how to design
873your presentation. The next one could be... signals during the presentation.
874You see what I'm doing here? So these are idea clusters that are then divided into videos.
875And these numbers, in my opinion, from my experience, work the best. Three to seven sections,
876three to seven videos, right? And what you want to do is, when you create those videos, you want to
877make sure that they are straight to the point. Remember, transformation versus just dumping content
878on people. This is super important. People don't have the time to watch 20, 30-minute videos.
879So what you want to do is you want to create videos in such a way that when people watch the first

881It's kind of like, have you ever seen, let's say, Game of Thrones or Narcos? Anybody? You know what

883.m.
884Cliffhanger, last minute, someone's about to die. What do you do? You carry on.
885All right 4 a.m. This is gonna be the last one But then your partner says no no we got to find out
886who's gonna die now, right? And so the next thing you know is 7 a.m. Now you can do this with your
887courses Of course, you don't make it tacky. You don't make it cheesy But you want to make it happen

897creating two quick win videos that they can implement right away. When you do that,

899the entire course because the course is only 45 to 90 minutes long. They complete it. They rave

901you get repeated customers, not by building a 10-hour behemoth that you probably will never
902publish. And then you want to add resources and you want to create updates. You want to create
903updates based on the feedback from your customers. Make sense? Yeah?

Brain Dump, Idea Clusters, Pyjama Draft

Lines 904–9991,675 words~11 minLanguage ×1
On-screen text
  • Start with the end in mind
  • Avatar → wins → brain dump → idea clusters → 80% curriculum → film
  • "You learn the most while filming."
  • The pyjama draft
  • iPhone 4 and a basic microphone
Editor notes

The longest single lesson of the day and the most-cited by past students. Keep the AI-as-avatar tip (921–926) and the crew-on-percentage story (985–993).

904Okay, amazing. So, you may ask, how do we even go about designing the content of my course?
905And so there is one really important principle. I'm going to give you a few steps here. Very
906simple, but tonight I want you to play with this. Okay, actually, no, we have a big party tonight.
Trim · lines 906–907 · "we have a big party tonight. Tomorrow, tomorrow" — live-only aside; keep "play with this".
907Tomorrow, tomorrow, tomorrow, play with this, okay? Always start with end in mind.
908Always reverse engineer whatever you want to create for your audience. This is so simple. I could
909give you some complicated diagram or framework, but it would make no sense. I want to keep it
910simple because I want you to do it ASAP. So first of all, you've got to figure out who is the
911avatar and what are the wins, the specific transformation that I want to give to this avatar.
912The avatar is here. You are here. You're ahead of the game. What you have to give to that person so
913you bring them closer to you. You've got to be very specific. Now, once you define that,
914you do the poetic thing of a brain dump. That's literally what you do. You think about everything

916story now some of you may be thinking come on mate i can just go into ai and it can build it for me

923feed all of the wins you feed all of the information about your avatar into that project in AI and
924you say hey listen I'll be building a course and I want you to stress test everything in the
925context of my customer avatar as we talk to that AI as if is that is your customer extremely
926powerful but don't be lazy and still talk to your actual avatar Nothing replaces talking to real
927people. Make sense so far? Yeah? Okay, next one. You want to create idea clusters.
928We talked about it. So you look at your brain dump. You look at your avatar, where you want to take
929that avatar. You ask yourself, if I was doing a live thing and I needed to have breaks in between,
930what would be the clusters? What's the first session? What's the second session? What's the third
931session? Maybe fourth and fifth, right? And these... Sessions, they become your idea clusters,
932meaning your sections. And then you put videos into those sections. It's actually very simple.
933You see, people overcomplicate course creation, but it's way more simple than people think.
934And here's an important thing. What you do next is you create your curriculum based on that.
935But you make it 80% good enough. Now, if you are a toxic perfectionist like me,
936This sounds scary. It took me a long time to actually learn this. I'm always the guy who's trying
937to make everything perfect. But you want to be 80% ready when you are building your curriculum.
938Otherwise, you will keep tweaking, you will keep redesigning. You will never launch, you will never
939learn from your audience. Again, as I mentioned before, you learn the most by launching your MVP
940and getting feedback from your audience. So when you feel like your curriculum is 80% ready,
941that's okay, just move on. Okay? Very, very important. And then, and this is important,
942you take all of this, the imperfect curriculum, and you film your MVP. Now,
943when I say film your MVP, some of you are probably feeling this feeling like, oh, filming already?
944Not enough steps. I need to do more. I need to make it better. But here's the thing. You learn the
945most while filming. This is why, for example, one of the worst ways to prepare for a presentation
946like this. It's just to write things down and just sit in your house and just... Right?
947No, you want to get out there, you want to talk, you want to go for a walk, you want to speak out
948loud, right? You want to activate your vocal cords, you want to see what sticks, what doesn't,
949what's awkward to say. Same thing applies to building a course. You want to film your MVP.

4.3

Build a Challenge Funnel: Promise, VIP, Benchmarks

Lines 1200–13061,782 words~12 min
On-screen text
  • A promise: believable · simple · stretched
  • "Gain 500 Instagram followers in five days"
  • Free community · VIP · product · upsell
  • 50% of big-ticket buyers bought the VIP first
  • Opt-in → upgrade → show-up sequence → challenge → sales sequence
  • Benchmarks: 40% opt-in · 4% VIP · 15% live / 30% total · 7% conversion
  • Free challenge vs paid challenge ($7 to $99)
Editor notes

Alessio's slides carry the benchmarks; rebuild them as clean cards.

1200Now, I always say that you need to use a challenge for different reasons. The first one is because
1201you can build an audience fast. Challenges are fun, are cool. People want to join and challenge
1202themselves.
1203And you can create a lot of engagement because people need to take action every single day.
1204They don't just learn and do nothing. they actually need to do something every single day, and it
1205creates a community. No other funnels create a community. A webinar doesn't create a community.
1206A challenge does, because you have a lot of people working together towards a goal, and they help
1207each other. They ask questions, they support each other. That's incredible if you are starting out
1208and you really want to take things to the next level. And, as I mentioned before, it's the perfect
1209law for selling. because you are already making a change in the people's life. You are narrowing
1210the gap. And obviously, because it's cool, because it's fun, it costs less than other funnels to
1211acquire leads, so that you can scale even faster. Now, let's go to the key,
1212the promise. The promise needs to have three characteristics. It needs to be believable,
1213it needs to be simple, and it needs to be stretched. Let's take an example of that.
1214So a bad challenge will be like, lose 20 pounds in 10 days. It's impossible.
1215No one would believe on that unless you're doing something crazy. But like, lose five pounds in 10
1216days, okay. Yeah, I believe I can do it. Probably I'm not able to do it alone,
1217but if someone held me and there's a roadmap, maybe I could do it. It doesn't change my life,
1218but if I lose these five pounds in 10 days, maybe I can get to my weight goal in six months,
1219right? Or you have something like, create your first business plan. Learn how to refine your idea.
1220Build a prototype for your product in seven days. It's too long to even forget what this is about.

1222better. And you can have something like post daily on Instagram for five days.
1223That's boring. Everyone can post on Instagram. You don't need a challenge for that, right? But then
1224you have something like gain 500 Instagram followers in five days. Now, that's interesting because
1225the mechanics. It might be the same. I just need to post every day some specific post. But when I

1227good. And don't get me wrong, 500 followers don't change anyone's life. But it's the first step
1228towards maybe getting that community that you need to launch your program or your services. So the
1229big question for you is what's your promise? If you think about your coaching, your courses,
1230the service, the product that you have, What's the gap that you have? And is there a promise that
1231you can deliver in a small amount of time that will prove them that you are the right person?
1232Because once you're able to do this, and once you deliver on the promise, half of the sales is
1233done.

The Law of Precision: Quiz Funnels

Lines 1306–13841,282 words~8.5 min
On-screen text
  • Law 2: Precision
  • A bad quiz collects data. A good quiz creates the customer.
  • The ARC: aspirational → reflective → the honesty question
  • 8 to 12 questions · 3 to 5 results · a page for each
  • Archetype · Diagnostic · Score · Potential · Risk
Editor notes

The quiz funnel needs no face on camera (1321–1324): say so on screen, it is the answer for viewers still carrying the Day 2 wound.

1306free challenge when you want to build your community. Now, let's go back to the basic.
Trim · Starts mid-line at “Now, let's go back to the basic.”; the words before it belong to the previous video or the cut.
1307We have here the classic basic of every single product and service,
1308where people are right now and where they will want to be. The reality of the fact is that this is
1309how it looks like for your customer. There are hundreds of ways to go where you need to go.
1310and then you follow an influencer that tells you to do something, and then you follow someone else
1311that tells you to do something else, and then you start something, and there is this huge confusion
1312that people have, and they don't know, okay, how do I go from where I am to where I need to be? Can
1313someone help me here? The reality is that all you need to do is to give them a specific path.
1314You need to convince them that what they tried till now didn't work because there wasn't a path
1315that was specific enough for them. And that's what we call the law of precision. We want to give a
1316precise path that really, really, really works for them. And once they get the right path, they can
1317go to the next level. And this is the quiz funnel. And quiz funnel are very simple.
1318They are basically a sales funnel where you diagnose where your customer actually is,
1319and you find the right path for them. And this right path is exactly what allows them to go to the
1320next level.
1321Using a quiz is very cool because a lot of you are funders, and a lot of you don't want to show
1322yourself yet. You're still working on your visibility wound, right? The quiz funnel doesn't need
1323you. It doesn't need you to show up and show your face. So you can actually start very well with a
1324quiz funnel. And you can simply ask questions, bring people in, separate them,
1325collect data on them, and then segment your list also based on the different answers that people
1326give you. And because chips are fun, it actually allows you to get a lot of cheap leads,
1327a lot of people that come in and will learn a lot from you and will want to continue working with
1328you. But there is a way to do a good quiz and a bad quiz. What most of all the people do,
1329probably 98%, is that they have a bad quiz. What they do is that they ask some questions that
1330identify the customer, collect data, and a lot of times they ask too many questions,
1331and you take 30 minutes to finish a quiz. Some other time, they do three questions,
1332so it's not enough for them to identify anything. And then they don't build the quiz to shift
1333something inside the customer.
1334They just ask questions. Most of all the time, those questions are even in third person, so it
1335doesn't feel personal. A good quiz is very different. The good quiz creates the customer.
1336It's about how you ask the question. You need to help someone know not just the outcome.
1337They know the outcome they want. the obstacle that's stopping them to achieve that outcome.
1338The flow of your question should follow what's called the arc. And the arc means that you basically
1339have the first questions that are asked in a positive and aspirational way, the middle questions
1340that are always in a reflective way, that you make them reflect about what they do and stuff, and
1341in the end, you need to have the honesty question. Those questions are the ones that need to make
1342them think, I need to do something about it. your quiz needs to shift them.
1343You need to use the first person, you need to make things very personal, and once you're able to
1344shift them through the quiz, they will be a lot more likely to then purchase a product from you.
1345And here is the thing, when you need to do a quiz, you need many things. You start with a
1346diagnostic hook, we're going to see a few examples of them, and then you need 8 to 12 questions.
1347No more, no less. And those questions need to follow the ARC system.
1348that I just explained. Then you need to have three to five different results that people can have,
1349and a personalized result page for each of the different answers. Now,
1350if you want to put your face out there, you can also put a video explaining the result on that
1351page, but a lot of people do it also without a video. Normally, video converts a bit better. And
1352then, last but not the least, a product or a service that you sell on the back end of this.
1353Here is how a typical quiz funnel works. You send people directly to a quiz page.
1354People answer the different questions. At the end, because they're so invested and they really know
1355that they need to take the next step, you ask them their name, email, and phone number. They opt
1356in, and then they receive a personalized result. And through this personalized result,
1357you upsell your product, and then you also do an email sequence that allow them to purchase your
1358main offer.
1359There are different types of quizzes.
1360And these are probably very interesting for different types of books that you can have.
1361And you can see also in this slide that it's not just for course creators, it's for any business.
1362You can have an archetype quiz. What's your money story? What's your money type?
1363And you give them a name to who they are.
1364And then you can have a diagnostic quiz, which is probably one of the most common type of quiz.
1365where you figure out what's the number one block in your practice. And you name the root cause of
1366their block. And based on this root cause, you give them a specific solution that works for them
1367and allow them to achieve the result. And then you score. For example, how aligned is your
1368business? How purposeful is your business? It's a score from 0 to 100, and then they have a number
1369that they need to improve. Oh my God, I'm just 55. What are the action items I need to do to arrive
1370there?
1371And then you have a potential. The potential, this is very important, especially even in brick and
1372mortar businesses, is like, how fast could you fill your practice?
1373And then you show the gap between where they are and where they could be. How much money can you
1374make if you actually go through this? And on the other hand, you have the risk, which is, is your
1375offer quietly repelling clients? And in this case, they actually are thinking,
1376oh my God, how much money am I leaving on the table every single day by not doing this? Now,
1377again, marketing is simple. It's all about understanding really where your customers are.
1378What Jimmy was saying before, customer avatar. Fully understand what they're doing, where they are.
1379Fully understand where they want to be, and then bridge the gap. You can do that.
1380by narrowing it with a lot of momentum, or you can do that by showing a lot of different roads and
1381give them the perfect path for them with a lot of precision.
1382But if you create your marketing in a smart way, you don't have to sell like crazy and be a used
1383car salesman. You can do it purposefully, you can create something incredible,
1384and you can take things to the next level.

Three Laws of the Ladder

Lines 1810–1861, 1864–19051,478 words~9.9 min
On-screen text
  • A ladder is a relationship architecture, not a menu
  • Law 1: every rung is a complete transformation
  • Law 2: the jump must feel inevitable, not pushy
  • Law 3: the top of the ladder is never a product. It's an identity.
  • $49 → $299 a year → $5,000 certification

2491today, I would have access to the best knowledge from Daniel Priestley to Vishen to be able to
2492build my next business or my next idea in a fraction of the time.
2493So let's talk about three rules. The first rule, and I think this is a rule which works on any
2494social media platform, is to build on pre-validated demand. And I want to introduce you to an idea
2495of something called an outlier. Hands up if you've heard of an outlier before.
2496So if you haven't heard of an outlier, an outlier is a video that's performed disproportionately
2497well relative to the average video on a given channel. And you can calculate an outlier score by
2498taking that particular video's number of views and dividing it by the average of the previous 10.
2499So, for example, if a video got 1.2 million views, but it's only used to getting 12,000 views,
2500that's a 100x outlier. And this was a term that was coined by Mr.
2501Beast's strategy team probably about five years ago. And at that time, it was a really new and
2502novel. and nowadays people are starting to clock on to it a lot more so it's just worth mentioning
2503to you guys that if all of your competitors and peers are studying outliers and learning from the
2504videos that are performing really well on YouTube so should you and so I'm gonna show you a quick
2505screenshot of my YouTube homepage from this morning for what it's worth and you'll notice that next
2506to all of the YouTube videos you'll see two things you'll see VPH,
2507and then you'll see a multiplier. VPH stands for the number of views that video is getting per
2508hour. But the thing I really want you to pay attention to is the multiple. And so a video with a
2509higher multiple represents a video that has performed disproportionately well relative to that
2510particular channel. So this is a really easy tool that you guys can install.
2511It's called vidIQ, spelt V-I-D-I-Q. And if you go to the Google Chrome Web Store,
2512you'll be able to install this as an extension on your web browser. And so now when you're on
2513YouTube and you're passively consuming information over there, you'll be able to keep an eye out on
2514like, wait, why did that video do so well? Or there's an idea around looks maxing.
2515Why is looks maxing suddenly becoming a big thing on YouTube? Is that something that I can be able
2516to speak to as well?
2517install the vidIQ tool and you're looking out for outliers, you'll passively start to absorb what
2518kind of videos are performing really well and why. But the most important thing about an outlier is
2519understanding why a particular video did so well.
2520One of my favorite examples of an outlier is this one over here. I still remember when it came up
2521on my feed. I wasn't even supposed to be watching YouTube videos. I was supposed to be working. And
2522I saw this come up and I was like, damn. 10 years as a therapist, this is what people regret most.
2523They all regret it. Of course I want to find out what that is. And when you look at this particular
2524thumbnail, and you look at this particular title, this isn't necessarily a famous therapist.
2525He is just a therapist that's worked for 10 years in the industry. I'm sure there are many of you
2526here today that have worked in your industry for 10 years or longer. But he found a format, he
2527found an outlier format that works really well for him, and he leaned into it.
2528Every video idea has a ceiling. This is the second thing I'd like to introduce you to.
2529So if you're operating in a space where there just aren't that many viewers, then you need to
2530accept and understand that there's going to be a ceiling to the number of views you can expect to
2531get. For example, one of the people who I work with is a guy called Ryan Dice, and he wants to make
2532YouTube content that reaches $10 million entrepreneurs. And he said, Saf, I'd love a YouTube video
2533that gets 100,000 views. I said, Ryan, every other competitor in a space that makes this topic
2534only gets like 5,000 views per YouTube video. And it makes sense, because how many $10 million
2535entrepreneurs are there in the world that happen to be on YouTube and consuming this kind of stuff?
2536So by understanding your TAM, you can then understand how broad do you want to go with your video
2537ideas. And generally speaking, if you want to get more views on YouTube, you have to go bigger with
2538the idea, and you have to appeal to beginners as well. That's the other thing. It's kind of the
2539uncomfortable truth. I think a lot of people want to reach just the experts, but you end up
2540narrowing yourself into a niche. So there is ways of being able to broaden ideas and draw in the
2541beginners, but also appeal to your ideal viewers at the same time. But truthfully,
2542copying outliers is, in my opinion, table stakes in 2026.

Build a List. Protect the Trust. Don't Start With the Book.

Lines 3001–3033, 3522–3538843 words~5.6 minLanguage ×2
On-screen text
  • Pregnant for nine months, then a one-year-old, then a teenager: the list outlives the niche
  • "Build a list and get data on your list and build loyalty with your list."
  • "I only recommend products if I have actually used the product myself."
  • "Don't start on the book. Nobody is going to buy your book if you don't have the architecture."
  • Content creation half of the week · architecture half of the week

Unverified figures spoken from stage

"Half a billion dollars" in masterclass sales (3121, 3260, 3353), "$100 million" from five masterclasses (3355), "$40 million" for the Jim Kwik masterclass (3382), "1.1 million followers" (407), Jimmy "six to seven hundred thousand" (76) versus "over 1 million" (651, 721), Maria Wendt "$21 million" (629, 1504), "30% of all posts are bots" (546). House rule: one number, one source. Carry these as the speaker's own words with attribution, or trim, on comms' call. None verifies in a base the team holds.

Profanity

Vishen uses the F-word deliberately and defends it (1794–1809). Jimmy: "build the damn thing", "holy shit". Decide keep, bleep or cut once for the whole programme; the brief marks each instance in the script view.
